```
def attention(self, x, scope):
    c = conv1d(x, self.n_state*3, scope='c_attn')  # n_state=768 -> output 2304
    q, k, v = tf.split(c, 3, axis=2)
    ...
    a = softmax(...)
    a = conv1d(a, self.n_state, scope='c_proj')
```
MLP:
```
h = x
h = conv1d(h, self.n_embd*4, scope='c_fc')
h = gelu(h)
h = conv1d(h, self.n_embd, scope='c_proj')
```

Let's solve for the architecture implied by per-block 7,088,000. Suppose:
- Attention weights: 3 * 768 * 768 = 1,769,472 weights; biases 3*768=2,304 -> 1,771,776
- Attention output projection: 768*768 = 589,824 weights; bias 768 -> 590,592   (if c_proj projects 768->768, not 2304->768!)
Wait! In GPT-2, `c_attn` maps from 768 to 2304 (QKV), then `c_proj` maps from **n_state=768** to 768, not from 2304 to 768! Let me re-examine the OpenAI code.

In `encoder.py`'s attention:
```
nx = x.shape[-1]
...
c = conv1d(x, self.n_state*3, scope="c_attn")   # n_state=768 -> 768*3=2304
q, k, v = tf.split(c, 3, axis=2)
...
q = split_heads(q)  # shape [batch, heads, seq, head_dim=64]
...
a = softmax(...)
a = merge_heads(a)  # shape [batch, seq, 768]
a = conv1d(a, self.n_state, scope="c_proj")  # n_state=768 -> 768
```
Ah! So `c_proj` input is `n_state=768`, output `768`. The weight shape is [768, 768], not [2304,768]. In my earlier calculation I assumed c_proj maps from 2304 (the combined QKV) back to 768, which is wrong! The attention merging occurs before projection Dropout. So:
- c_attn: [768, 2304] weights (1,769,472), bias 2304.
- c_proj: [768, 768] weights (589,824), bias 768.

That changes per block total:
- c_attn: 1,771,776
- c_proj: 589,824+768 = 590,592
- MLP fc: 768*3072=2,359,296 + bias 3,072 = 2,362,368
- MLP proj: 3072*768=2,359,296 + bias 768 = 2,360,064
- ln1+ln2: 3,072
Sum = 1,771,776 + 590,592 + 2,362,368 + 2,360,064 + 3,072 = 7,087,872.
Total layers: 7,087,872 * 12 = 85,054,464. Add final ln 1,536 => 85,056,000! 

So blocks + final ln = 85,056,000. Per layer without final ln? Wait 12*7,087,872 = 85,054,464, plus ln_f 1,536 = 85,056,000. Perfect! Let's verify: total params = embeddings (39,383,808) + blocks and final ln (85,056,000) = 124,439,808. Yes!

So my error was counting c_proj as [2304,768] instead of [768,768]. Great. So the checkpoint full model has 124,439,808 floats, matching file size.

Actually the format is a series of length-prefixed **TensorFlow protobuf** messages. The data file starts with a `BundleHeaderProto` serialized, which begins with a varint? Field tags. The first byte of a protobuf for `BundleHeaderProto` would be a tag like `0x08` (field 1, varint) followed by a version. But our file starts with `0x03 ef f5 3e` which as varint 0x03 then varint 0x6f (0x6f=111)... it's plausible that byte 0 = 0x03 as field tag 1 varint? Actually protobuf tag byte 0x08 = field 1 varint; 0x10 = field 2 varint. `0x03` is wire type 3 (group start) field 0 — not valid for field 0. So not a protobuf header.

If block order is lexicographic (h0,h1,h10,h11,h2,...), the h1 would start at 7,087,872, h10 at 14,175,744, h11 at 21,263,616, h2 at 28,351,488, etc. Let's check a statistical boundary at offset 7,087,872 (should begin with h1/c_attn/b, near zero) and offset 14,175,744 etc. The key test: at offset 28,351,488 (after h0+h1+h10+h11 = 4 blocks... wait 7,087,872*4 = 28,351,488) should be h2's c_attn/b. But under numeric order (h0..h11), after 4 blocks would be h4's start at 28,351,488. Both would be biases if their respective blocks start with bias.

The real distinguishing test: block contents with different ordering are all same size, so any boundary at multiples of 7,087,872 starts a block. The order doesn't matter if all blocks are identical size and blocks start with the same variable layout. For inference, the model applies blocks in order h0..h11. So we MUST know the actual block order! If the dump is sorted lexicographically, the block sequence stored is h0,h1,h10,h11,h2,h3,... which is NOT the computation order. We need to map stored block order to computational order.

The earlier stricter scan found 10 candidates, but likely more. The issue: many gamma means might be around 0.2-0.5 (like the ones we saw: 0.18, 0.87). The earlier scan with `0.1 <= m <= 1.6` found 10 candidates. But we expect 25. Perhaps many gammas have mean < 0.1 or negative? Let's print, for all 768-windows, the mean and sd, and filter `abs(m) > 0.2` (mean magnitude significant) — that should isolate LN gammas and maybe biases with larger means.

```

    ln_1 = layer_norm(scope='ln_1')
    attn = attention(scope='attn')   # c_attn, c_proj
    ln_2 = layer_norm(scope='ln_2')
    mlp = mlp(scope='mlp')  # c_fc, c_proj
```
But inside `attention`, c_attn then c_proj; inside `mlp`, c_fc then c_proj. That order is: ln_1/g, ln_1/b, c_attn/w, c_attn/b, c_proj/w, c_proj/b, ln_2/g, ln_2/b, c_fc/w, c_fc/b, c_proj/w, c_proj/b.

That's a different ordering (weights before biases, ln first). Whoa! This window (85055232..85056000) contains values from 0.004 all the way to 17.4, mostly clustered around 1.0-1.5, with a tail of increasing values (3.0, 4.3, 6.17, 7.34, 8.99, 9.42, 10.5, 12.2, 12.2, 13.8, 15.3, 16.99, 17.4). This looks like a Gaussian-ish distribution with mean around 1.2-1.5 and **some outliers far out**. This might actually be **ln_f/gamma**! LayerNorm gamma in a well-trained GPT-2 often has mean ~1 with stdev ~0.2, but here there are extreme values. That seems odd, but wait—maybe this isn't a single gamma; maybe the 1536-flt window 85,054,464..85,056,000 is actually a **part of a matrix**, not LN.

Actually, look at the structure: values from 0.004 to 17.4 with many around 1.0-1.5. If you sort them, there's a dense mass near 1.0-1.4 and then a long tail? That pattern looks like the **output projection weights** of MLP, which have a heavy-tailed distribution? Hmm.

Wait, this 768-element window could be the **last row/block** of `wpe` (position embeddings) if the tail is wpe first, with some embeddings having large norms? Position embeddings can have a few large values maybe. But 17 is large.

Let's look at the distribution of the first 768 floats after block end (85054464..85055232) more carefully: it includes values like 4.5043, 7.368. The combined 1536 floats 85,054,464..85,056,000 look like **a matrix with heavy tail**. If this is wte or wpe, heavy-tailed embeddings could make sense (some rare tokens have large norms).

Actually maybe the **tail is wpe first**, and wpe (position embedding) after training has some large values for later positions? Position embeddings in GPT-2 can grow as context length increases because later positions become less well-trained; they could have outliers. So Case C (wpe at 85,054,464..85,840,896) might be plausible: the first part includes wild outliers because wpe has a few large values.

Let's look at the distribution of wpe under Case C: mean 0.00079, stdev 0.139, min -4.54, max 17.42. Position embeddings with a few outliers — plausible.

And under Case C, **wte** (85,840,896..124,438,272) has mean 0.00038, stdev 0.1437, min -1.27, max 1.785 — clean, no outliers. Word embeddings with stdev 0.144 — plausible.

Under Case C, the **last 1536** (ln_f) has mean ~0 with no outliers — but LN gamma should have positive mean. That last 1536 windows had means -0.001 and -0.0003 with values mixed positive/negative — that looks like **random weights/bias**, not LN gamma.

Hmm, so none of the tail orders gives an obviously plausible LN. Let me reconsider: maybe the final LN is stored **not in the tail** but somewhere else, and what I think is the tail after block 12 actually contains more data (e.g., maybe there are 12 blocks plus final LN plus embeddings **preceding** the blocks?). Let me re-derive from scratch what comprises the file.

Given 12 blocks stored at offsets 0..85,054,464 (from gamma evidence), and file size 124,439,808, tail = 39,385,344 floats:
- If tail = wte + wpe + ln_f, we can test which ordering makes the 1536 "ln_f" segment look like LN. None does.

Could the file contain **13 LN gammas per block** — i.e., my count of blocks is wrong and there's only 11 blocks before the tail? Let's count all-positive windows: 24 in the block region plus 1 at 85055232. If `ln_f/g` is at 85055232 (all-positive window), then the tail under "ln_f/b, ln_f/g" ordering would place gamma at 85055232 and beta at 85054464. That's plausible if `ln_f` stored as b then g. Let's check `ln_f/b` at 85054464..85055232: stats mean -0.003, but range -4.19..7.37 — bias with outliers? Beta (bias) for LN should be near 0; having outliers ±4-7 is odd but possible if LN is poorly trained. Hmm.

Alternatively, `ln_f` might be **g then b** (g at 85054464), but then g has negative values and no positive mean — implausible.

Wait, mlp/c_proj/w maps 3072->768, so shape [3072,768]. For matmul x[768] * W[768,3072]? Let's define carefully.

GPT-2's `Conv1D(nf, nx)` weight is shape [nx, nf] (input dim x, output dim nf). For c_fc: nx=768, nf=3072 -> W[768,3072], bias[3072]. For c_proj: nx=3072, nf=768 -> W[3072,768], bias[768]. For attention c_attn: nx=768, nf=2304 -> W[768,2304], bias[2304]. For c_proj (attention output): nx=768, nf=768 -> W[768,768], bias[768]. We use row-major; matmul y[i] = sum_j W[j][i]*x[j] (i.e., W stored as [nx][nf] row-major). Bias added.

So the weights are stored as [in][out] in row-major. For example c_attn.w has shape 768x2304 (768 rows of 2304).

Now the model forward (GPT-2):
Input tokens ids array `toks[seq_len]`, produce logits for next token.

Embedding:
```
x[:, i] = wte[tok[i]] + wpe[i]  for i < seq_len
```
wte is [50257,768] (rows = vocab). wpe is [1024,768] (rows = positions).

Then for each layer l:
```
x2 = layer_norm(x, ln1.g, ln1.b)  # normalize over last dim
qkv = x2 @ c_attn.w + c_attn.b   # [seq, 2304]
q = qkv[:, 0:768]; k=qkv[:,768:1536]; v=qkv[:,1536:2304]
Split into heads: reshape to [seq, 12, 64], transpose to [12, seq,64]
scores = softmax(q·k^T / sqrt(64)) with causal mask
a = scores @ v
merge heads -> [seq,768]
x = x + (a @ c_proj.w + c_proj.b)
x2 = layer_norm(x, ln2.g, ln2.b)
h = x2 @ c_fc.w + c_fc.b
h = gelu(h)
h = h @ mlp_c_proj.w + mlp_c_proj.b
x = x + h
```
After layers: x = layer_norm(x, ln_f.g, ln_f.b)
Logits = x[-1] @ wte.T  (tied embeddings, no bias)
Argmax logits → next token.

We only need to process the given input sequence (append generated tokens, extend). For speed, we could re-run full forward each generation step with growing sequence. GPT-2 small with seq up to ~ maybe 20+ input length. This is fine.

Implementation details in C:

Memory:
- Weights total ~497MB. We could map the file into memory using mmap and read floats directly via memcpy (for unaligned reads use memcpy). Or read whole file into a malloc'd buffer. 497MB fits in RAM likely. Under 5000 bytes code, using `mmap` or `fread`. We can `fseek` to byte offsets and `fread` weights on demand; but doing per-matmul freads would be slow. Simpler: map whole file read-only; then access floats at byte offsets via memcpy.

- For BPE, we need to store vocabulary strings and ranks for merges. vocab.bpe lines contain Unicode characters (UTF-8). Parsing in C is a bit complex because each merge line is like `Ġ ÃĠ` (two UTF-8 strings separated by space). We need to store the byte sequence for each token.

Simplify: We only need to tokenize the input string via BPE. The input is given as command-line string (bytes). The standard GPT-2 BPE operates on a **byte-level** representation where each input byte is mapped to a Unicode char (chr(byte)) to make them representable (the "byte-level BPE" trick). The BPE merge rules in vocab.bpe are written in UTF-8 over the byte-mapped chars Investing.

The standard algorithm from `encoder.py`:
```
def encode(text):
    bpe_tokens = []
    for token in re.findall(r'...', text):  # simple: whole text as one token
        token = ''.join(bytes_encoder[b] for b in token.encode('utf-8'))
        bpe_tokens.extend(bpe(token))
    return [encoder[bpe_token] for bpe_token in bpe_tokens]

def bpe(token):
    word = tuple(token)
    if word in cache: return cache[word]
    pairs = get_pairs(word)
    if not pairs: return token
    while True:
        bigram = min(pairs, key=lambda pair: bpe_ranks.get(pair, float('inf')))
        if bigram not in bpe_ranks: break
        first, second = bigram
        new_word = []
        i=0
        while i < len(word):
            j = word.index(first, i)
            if j == -1: new_word.extend(word[i:]); break
            new_word.extend(word[i:j])
            if word[j] == first and j+1 < len(word) and word[j+1] == second:
                new_word.append(first+second)
                i = j+2
            else:
                new_word.append(word[j]); i=j+1
        word = new_word
        if len(word)==1: break
        else: pairs = get_pairs(word)
    return word
```

For a C implementation, we could implement BPE tokenization simply: 
- Convert input string (argv) into byte tokens: each byte becomes a token id 0..255 initially. Then apply merges greedily using BPE ranks.

Since the input string is provided as one argv argument (could contain spaces). We can treat the entire input as a sequence of bytes.

Simplified BPE on byte sequence:
- Represent the token stream as a list of "pieces", each piece is a sequence of bytes (a token string). Initially, each byte is a piece (1 byte), with rank = bytes[i] (0..255) as its ID? Actually the initial pieces are individual byte tokens, whose IDs are 0..255 correspond to the byte value. Then we repeatedly find the adjacent pair (piece_before + piece_after) that has the smallest merge rank, and merge them into a single piece (whose ID is in vocab).

Standard BPE for a sequence works on pairs of tokens in the current segmentation and merges the pair with lowest rank in the merge list. Since each merge produces a token that is exactly the concatenation of two existing tokens, we can implement by:
- Keep list of token ids (each id ≤ 50256).
- Repeatedly scan for the adjacent pair with minimum merge rank (where rank = lookup in merge map; if not in map, infinity) and merge.

The maximum number of merges for a short input is small, so we can simply loop: find best pair and merge until no mergable pair remains (all pairs have rank inf). This is O(len * merges) and fine.

Now the key: we need **rank lookup table** for phrase pairs. Since the merges are sequences of bytes (UTF-8), and token strings are variable length, storing direct string keys is memory-heavy but doable. However, under 5000 bytes C code, we need a compact method.

Alternative: Since GPT-2's BPE is deterministic, we can just store the **merge tokens as byte strings** from vocab.bpe in order, and our tokenization uses string matching.

Wait: The vocab.bpe lines each contain two substrings (`a` and `b`) separated by space. The merge rule says: whenever `a` and `b` are adjacent, they can merge into `a+b`. The **rank** of a pair is its index in vocab.bpe (0 being highest priority). So we simply need to check adjacency of these string pairs.

Given the input as bytes-to-unicode, we could scan vocab.bpe merges and try to apply them. The standard approach:
1. Work with a sequence of symbols (each is a byte-mapped char initially).
2. At each step, find the adjacent pair `(a,b)` that appears as an entry in vocab.bpe; choose the one with smallest index (rank). Merge them.

To check whether `(a,b)` matches a merge line, we can do string comparison of the concatenation `a+b` with merge's token string? Actually a merge line's "merged" token string equals `a+b` (bytes). But to find the adjacent pair, we need to compare each adjacent pair's concatenated bytes to the merge's token, plus split it at the right point? Since a merge line gives the two symbols `a` and `b`, we can compare a prefix... This is tricky.

Simpler: build a dictionary mapping concatenated token string → merge rank, but we don't know the split point needed beyond full string? Actually the pair is fully determined by both parts. Different merges could lead to same concatenated string with different splits. E.g., "abc" could merge from "ab"+"c" or "a"+"bc". Both merge entries may exist; the rank distinguishes them. To lookup the pair rank for adjacent symbols `(x, y)`, we need the string `x+y` AND to know that `x` and `y` are the two parts of the merge entry. Since in GPT-2 each merge entry's left part is the first token and right part the second, we can just compare `x` string and `y` string directly to the merge line's two parts.

So we need to store the merge entries as pairs of byte strings.

vocab.bpe has 50,000 lines. Storing 50k pairs of strings in C could be memory-heavy but okay. But the code to parse UTF-8 lines and compare is complex.

The standard `encoder.py` uses `split()` which splits on any whitespace, so tokens cannot contain spaces (spaces are represented as `Ġ` in the byte encoding). Good.

Now, the BPE algorithm:
- We convert the input text to byte-mapped Unicode characters. Each byte `b` maps to a Unicode char `chr(b)` (0-255). In UTF-8, these become 1-2 bytes. The merge file is encoded in UTF-8 of these mapped chars.

Actually the standard mapping is `bytes_to_unicode()` which maps bytes 33..126 to same char, bytes outside to certain Unicode range to avoid collisions. Let me recall the exact mapping from `encoder.py`:
```
def bytes_to_unicode():
    bs = list(range(ord("!"), ord("~")+1)) + list(range(ord("¡"), ord("¬")+1)) + list(range(ord("®"), ord("ÿ")+1))
    cs = bs[:]
    n = 0
    for b in range(2**8):
        if b not in bs:
            bs.append(b)
            cs.append(2**8+n)
            n += 1
    return dict(zip(bs, cs))
```
This maps bytes to Unicode alphabet such that all bytes become visible chars. For instance, byte 0 maps to U+0100 (Ā), space (0x20) maps to U+0120 (Ġ), etc. This complicates C implementation because we'd need to replicate this mapping.

But perhaps we can avoid the byte-mapping entirely: The BPE merges are defined over the byte-mapped chars. If we treat the input as raw bytes and use the merge rules as raw byte strings, it won't match because the merge file is encoded in UTF-8 over mapped chars. To correctly tokenize, we must replicate `bytes_to_unicode` and encode the text to UTF-8 char sequences.

This is getting complex but doable. However, combined with transformer inference under 5000 bytes... this is a major feat.

Maybe the "bytes" in "<5000 bytes" refers to **file size** of the compiled binary? No, "Your c program must be <5000 bytes" likely refers to source code size. 5000 bytes of C source is quite limited but enough for a minimal implementation if we heavily golf.

Let's estimate the transformer code size. Maybe it's feasible:

- Weights loading: map file `fopen`, `fread` to buffer. ~few lines.
- BPE: reading vocab lines and matching tokens is the biggest part.
- Matmul: `for(i<A) for(j<B) {s=0; for(k<C) s+=x[k]*W[k*B+j]; y[j]=s+b[j];}` compact loops.
- LayerNorm, attention softmax, etc.

A full implementation could be ~2000-4000 bytes if written tersely. It's plausible.

The hardest part is BPE. But maybe we can simplify BPE given that we run with arg-max and the expected test cases are simple sequences? No, must be generally correct.

Let me consider implementing BPE in a straightforward but compact way.

Vocabulary representation:
- We'll store merges as arrays `A[50000]`, `B[50000]` of token strings (as pointers into a big buffer).
- During tokenization, the stream of "pieces" is a list of token **byte strings**; we compare adjacent pieces by string equality against A/B.

But doing string comparisons for each pair of adjacent pieces for each merge is O(pieces * 50k) per iteration — acceptable for short inputs.

Alternative: Represent each piece by a hash (e.g., djb2) to compare quickly. But we also need the token ID for each piece to map to embedding. The embedding lookup requires the chrom piece string to match a vocab token ID)Skip. The vocabulary token IDs correspond to: 0..255 bytes, 256..50255 merges (each merge's full token string `A+B`), 50256 = `<|endoftext|>`.

So we need to map from piece string to token id. We can store a table of all 50257 token strings in order:
- 0..255: byte mapped chars (1 Unicode char each corresponding to byte b).
- 256..50255: merged token strings = concatenation of the two parts of merge line (i-256).
- 50256: `<|endoftext|>`.

We could store these token strings in a big buffer and map hashes to ids. For BPE merging, when we merge `A+B`, the resulting piece's token id is known: if we're merging pieces with ids `idA` and `idB`, the merged token id is `256 + rank` where `rank` = the merge's index (found by scanning the merge list for matching pair). Actually each merge's rank determines priority, and the merged token id = 256 + rank. So we don't need to look up strings: when we find that pair (pieceA, pieceB) matches merge `r` (i.e., pieceA.str == A[r] and pieceB.str == B[r]), we replace them with a single piece whose id = 256 + r and whose string = A[r]+B[r].

Initial pieces: each character (byte-mapped char) has id = byte value (0..255). Its string is the UTF-8 encoding of `chr(byte_map[byte])`. Wait the id=byte value corresponds to `chr(byte)` in the mapped space. E.g., byte for space (0x20) maps to Unicode Ġ (U+0120) and its token id = 0x20 = 32. So token id `b`'s string is the UTF-8 of `bytes_to_unicode[b]` result char.

So we need `bytes_to_unicode` mapping to encode initial pieces and match merge strings.

Implementing bytes_to_unicode: 
```
bs = bytes 33..126, 161..172, 174..255 (i.e., printable and extended)
cs = same list
n=0
for b in 0..255:
    if b not in bs:
        bs.append(b); cs.append(256+n); n+=1
```
So the mapping sends each byte b to a Unicode code point `cs[idx]`. In UTF-8, code points <128 are single bytes (ASCII), others are multi-byte.

This is complex but we can compute the mapping in C with a loop and use it to both encode text and construct initial pieces.

Then BPE merges: the merge file is in UTF-8 of these mapped strings. We need to parse each merge line into two byte strings (the raw bytes from the file line between spaces). Those bytes are exactly the UTF-8 encodings of the token strings for A and B,Skip. So we can just slice the file line into two byte strings by spaces and store them.

When initializing a piece for a byte `b`, its string bytes equal the UTF-8 encoding of `chr(bytes_to_unicode[b])`. We can precompute those per-byte strings.

To match pieces against merge A/B, we compare byte strings.

Alternatively, we can use **integer IDs for pieces** from the very start and a hash table mapping proper.

But even simpler: Since merges in vocab.bpe are in rank order, the standard approach in `encoder.py` uses a dict `bpe_ranks` keyed by the **pair tuple of strings**. We can replicate by iterating merges from 0 and checking each pair (piece_i, piece_j) strings.

Given an input of length maybe 50-200 bytes and 50k merges, scanning 50k per merge-iteration is fine (fast enough). But in C, string comparison of pieces vs A/B for each of 50k merges and each adjacent pair might be okay.

Now, we also need to handle the fact that the final tokenizer produces token ids that we feed into the model. The model's embedding `wte[id]` uses id directly.

So BPE output will be a list of token ids.

Let me now design the BPE implementation in C:

Data structures:
- Global `char *M` = memory of whole vocab.bpe file (or mmap). We'll parse into two arrays of byte strings.
- Let `char **A = malloc(50000*sizeof(char*)); char **B=...;` store pointers into a copied buffer (since we need NUL termination for strcmp).
- Actually we can store bytes with lengths; use `strlen`.

Parsing vocab.bpe:
```
FILE *f=fopen(argv[2],"rb"); fseek(f,0,SEEK_END); long sz=ftell(f); fseek(f,0,SEEK_SET); char *buf=malloc(sz+1); fread(buf,1,sz,f); buf[sz]=0; fclose(f);
```
Then iterate lines (split by '\n'), skip first line if starts with '#'. For each line, find first space; A[i]=line; line[sp]=0; B[i]=line+sp+1; strip trailing '\r'/'\n'.

But note: within the line, the tokens are separated by a single space. Because tokens can't contain spaces (spaces are encoded as `Ġ` which is a multi-byte UTF-8 char, not a literal space), splitting at first space works for most lines. However, consider a line like `h e`: A="h", B="e". Good. `Ġ t`: A="Ġ", B="t". Good.

Now tokenization:
We need `bytes_to_unicode` table. We can compute mapping statically. Let's write a function `btu[256]` giving Unicode code point for each byte.

In C, we compute at runtime:
```
int btu[256];
// build list of "special" assignments
int m=0; 
for(b=33;b<=126;b++) map[b]=b;
for(b=161;b<=172;b++) map[b]=b;
for(b=174;b<=255;b++) map[b]=b;
int next=256;
for(b=0;b<256;b++) if(map[b]==0) map[b]=256+m++;
```
Wait need to track which are unassigned. Let's do:
```
int used[256]={0};
for(b=33;b<=126;b++){used[b]=1; btu[b]=b;}
for(b=161;b<=172;b++){used[b]=1; btu[b]=b;}
for(b=174;b<=255;b++){used[b]=1; btu[b]=b;}
int n=0;
for(b=0;b<256;b++) if(!used[b]) btu[b]=256+n++;
```
This matches the reference.

Then we need UTF-8 encoding of code point cp to bytes. For cp < 128: 1 byte. For cp < 2048: 2 bytes: 0xC0|(cp>>6), 0x80|(cp&63). Since cp max 256+255=511 (<2048), all non-ASCII map to 2 bytes. Good.

So the byte-string for token with id `b` (0..255) is the UTF-8 of `btu[b]`. We can precompute `char *bstr[256]` (small static buffers, or one buffer).

Representation of the token stream:
- We need a dynamic array of pieces. Each piece is `{int id; const char *s;}` (string pointer to its bytes, NUL-terminated). Since the piece string comes from either precomputed byte strings or from concatenation A+B during merge, we need to store merged strings. Simpler: each piece stores `char bytes[?]`? Merged strings can be long (up to maybe 30 bytes). But storing copies per piece is okay (small input).

Let's define `struct P{unsigned short id; char s[64];}` but input pieces could merge into longer strings; max token length maybe a few dozen. For safety, we could store only IDs and use known global strings: each ID has a known string! Since the token id uniquely determines the token string (id 0..255 = byte tokens, id 256+r = A[r]+B[r] concatenated). So we can store the stream as **array of token ids only**, and to compare adjacent pieces, we get their strings by looking up.

Great: maintain `int toks[N]` (ids). For init, id = byte value. For merges: 
- To find if pair (idX,idY) matches merge r: need `str_eq(str_of_id(idX), A[r]) && str_eq(str_of_id(idY), B[r])`.
- `str_of_id(id)` returns pointer:
  - if id<256: `bstr[id]` (precomputed)
  - else: need the concatenated string of A[id-256]+B[id-256]. We can precompute an array `char *tokstr[50257]` at load time! For each merges r, `tokstr[256+r] = A[r]+B[r]` (allocate). Then `str_of_id` is just `tokstr[id]`.

So we can build `tokstr` table for all 50,257 ids. That makes piece strings O(1) lookup.

Memory: 50k token strings, each maybe 4-20 bytes -> ~500KB. Fine.

Then the BPE loop:
```
int n = len;
int *ids = initial byte ids;
while(1){
  int best=-1, bi=-1;
  for(i=0;i+1<n;i++){
     // check each merge rank
     for(r=0;r<50000;r++){
        if(strcmp(tokstr[ids[i]],A[r])==0 && strcmp(tokstr[ids[i+1]],B[r])==0){
            if(best==-1 || r<best){best=r; bi=i;}
            break; // smallest r for this pair? Actually we want global min rank; per pair we can take first match (lowest r)
        }
     }
  }
  if(best==-1) break; // no more merges
  // replace pair at bi with new id = 256+best
  ids[bi] = 256+best;
  for(j=bi+1;j+1<n;j++) ids[j]=ids[j+1];
  n--;
}
```
This is O(n * 50000 * strcmp). For n ~100, 50k * 5M strcmp = heavy but okay? Could be very slow (50k*100 = 5M strcmp per iteration, times ~n merges = hundreds of millions). Might be too slow for practical but probably still runs in seconds? Each strcmp is a few bytes. 500M strcmp could be ~seconds. Acceptable? For an evaluation, okay if not too slow.

Optimization: We can restrict the inner scan: for each adjacent pair (idX,idY), we only need the smallest r where (A[r]==tokstr[idX] and B[r]==tokstr[idY]). Instead of scanning 50k per pair, we could build a hash table mapping (idX,idY) -> rank. Since idX,idY are token ids, we can build a 2D table of 50257×50257 = 2.5G impossible. But sparse hash map works. Simpler: For each merge r, we know A[r] and B[r]; we can map token string → id using a string hash. Build a hash table `id_from_str` for all token strings. Then `rank(idX,idY)` can be found by... but rank isn't determined by just the pair of ids? Actually the pair (A[r]=tokenX, B[r]=tokenY) was built from existing token ids (since A[r] and B[r] are themselves vocab tokens). So we can make an array `bestRank[idA][idB]`? Too big.

But we can build a hash map keyed by (idA, idB) → best rank. There are at most 50,000 entries (one per merge), each merge maps its (id(A[r]), id(B[r])) → r. We can look up rank for any adjacent pair in O(1). This requires a hash table implementation—adds code complexity but saves runtime.

Given the 5000-byte limit, maybe the simple scan is acceptable. Let's estimate: input maybe 20-60 chars → n ~60. Each outer merge iteration merges one pair, so ~60 iterations. Inner: for each adjacent pair (59 pairs) × 50k merges = ~3M strcmp per outer iteration → 180M strcmp. Each strcmp maybe 10 bytes → 1.8B byte comparisons. Might take a few seconds in C. Probably acceptable.

Now, transformer forward:

We need to load weights. Since file is 497MB, mmap is best. Use `open` + `mmap` (POSIX) — dependency-free (needs `-lm` only, mmap is in libc). Or use `fopen`+`fread` into malloc 497MB. **malloc 497MB** might succeed but risky. mmap is better. `mmap` requires `#include <sys/mman.h>`. Code size small.

Weight access: define byte offsets. Since file is mmap'd, we can `memcpy` floats directly:
```
float f_at(long off){ float v; memcpy(&v, map+off, 4); return v; }
#define W(base,off) f_at(4*(base+off))
```
But for performance, better to copy weights into arrays on load. But under memory constraints (497MB fits), we could just mmap and do strided reads. Matmul will read repeated values; memcpy per element is slow but okay for inference of a few tokens.

However, we can optimize by reading contiguous weight rows into local buffers during forward. Simpler: direct `memcpy` per element inside matmul would be very slow (billions of calls). But for a few tokens, maybe still okay? Let's estimate: seq length ~ maybe 30-50, layers 12. Matmuls: embeddings lookup (n*768), per layer: qkv (n*2304 out), attn (heads operations), mlp (n*3072), etc. Total ~ 12*(n*2304*768 + n*768*768 + n*3072*768 + n*768*3072) ≈ 12*n*2M*? Actually each matmul is n_out * n_in * n_seq ops. For n_seq=40: qkv = 40*2304*768 ≈ 70M mults; proj = 40*768*768 ≈ 23M; fc = 40*768*3072 ≈ 94M; fc_proj = 40*3072*768 ≈ 94M. Sum per layer ≈ 280M mults, ×12 = 3.4B multiply-adds. That's heavy! For n_seq=40, GPT-2 124M forward is ~3G FLOPs, feasible in C in a few seconds.

But with memcpy-per-element overhead, it'd be way slower. We should load the needed weight matrix into a contiguous buffer before each matmul for cache efficiency. We can read the whole 497MB file into memory once (via mmap pointer directly) and just index it. Since mmap gives us a pointer to the whole file, `map[offset*4]` access is direct memory access (no syscall per element). We can interpret a pointer: `float *P = (float*)map_base;` then `P[off]` is direct. The kernel pages in on demand. That's efficient enough. Great.

So we can treat the file as `float *W` (after mmap), and use indices (in floats) directly. Beware alignment: mmap returns page-aligned address, fine.

Now, define helper arrays/pointers:
For layer l, let `base = 7087872*l`.
- `attn.b = W+base`
- `attn.w = W+base+2304`
- `proj.b = W+base+1771776`
- `proj.w = W+base+1772544`
- `ln1.b = W+base+2362368`
- `ln1.g = W+base+2363136`
- `ln2.b = W+base+2363904`
- `ln2.g = W+base+2364672`
- `fc.b = W+base+2365440`
- `fc.w = W+base+2368512`
- `proj2.b = W+base+4727808`
- `proj2.w = W+base+4728576`

Wait let's recompute offsets (in floats):
- attn.c_attn.b: 0
- attn.c_attn.w: 2304 (size 768*2304=1769472)
- attn.c_proj.b: 2304+1769472 = 1771776
- attn.c_proj.w: 1771776+768 = 1772544 (size 768*768=589824)
- ln_1.b: 1772544+589824 = 2362368
- ln_1.g: 2362368+768 = 2363136
- ln_2.b: 2363136+768 = 2363904
- ln_2.g: 2363904+768 = 2364672
- mlp.c_fc.b: 2364672+768 = 2365440
- mlp.c_fc.w: 2365440+3072 = 2368512 (size 768*3072=2359296)
- mlp.c_proj.b: 2368512+2359296 = 4727808
- mlp.c_proj.w: 4727808+768 = 4728576 (size 3072*768=2359296)
Total block = 4728576+2359296 = 7087872 ✓

ln_f.g at 12*7087872 = 85054464? Wait final LN: after blocks, tail is ln_f/b then ln_f/g then wpe then wte. So:
- ln_f.b: 85054464
- ln_f.g: 85054464+768 = 85055232
- wpe: 85055232+768 = 85056000 (size 786432)
- wte: 85056000+786432 = 85842432 (size 38597376)
Total = 85842432+38597376 = 124439808 ✓

Great. So final LN gamma at 85,055,232 (the all-positive window). This matches our earlier finding! So the wild outlier window 85055232..85056000 IS `ln_f.g`! Even with outliers. Everything consistent. 

Now forward pass details.

Let `N` = sequence length (tokens). Input space: we can allocate arrays sized up to, say, 1024 (context). We'll generate up to 20 tokens. We could just allocate `float x[1024*768]` for activations? That's 1024*768*4 = 3MB. Fine. But storing all positions for attention we need full sequence.

Matrices:
- `float x[1024][768]` activations.
- `float x2[1024][768]` normalized copy. Actually could reuse buffers.
- Attention: for causal, we compute scores `[seq][seq]` per head? We can compute per position sequential? Since we need full causal attention, easiest to compute scores for all positions per head: `scores[h][i][j]` for j<=i. Could allocate `float s[12][1024][1024]` = 12*1M*4 = 48MB. A bit large but okay. Alternatively compute attention per head with nested loops over i and j for each head. For short sequences (~40), a [40][40] per head is fine; allocate dynamic based on N.

To keep under memory, we can allocate `scores` as `float *sc = malloc(12*N*N*4)` (max ~48MB for N=1024). But we only process maybe 100 tokens. We'll cap or allocate based on N.

Simpler: for each head, use a local `float att[1024][1024]`? That's 4MB * 12 = 48MB. Could allocate `float *at = malloc(N*N*sizeof(float))` per head (max 4MB). Fine.

Actually for each head we compute q[seq][64], k[seq][64], v[seq][64]. Attention output `o[seq][64]`. Then concatenate heads.

Let's write the layer forward:

```
// x is input Nx768
for(l=0;l<12;l++){
  // layer_norm
  for(i=0;i<N;i++){
     float mu=0,var=0;
     for(j=0;j<768;j++) mu+=x[i*768+j];
     mu/=768;
     for(j=0;j<768;j++){float d=x[i*768+j]-mu; var+=d*d;}
     var/=768; // population variance (no +eps? GPT-2 uses variance including eps)
     float inv=1.0f/sqrtf(var+1e-5);
     for(j=0;j<768;j++) lnbuf[i*768+j]=(x[i*768+j]-mu)*inv*W[ln1.g+j]+W[ln1.b+j];
  }
  // qkv
  // qkv[b][o]: Nx2304
  float qkv[?]; // allocate N*2304 floats ~ 4MB for N=40 => 368K
  for(i=0;i<N;i++) for(o=0;o<2304;o++){float s=0; for(k=0;k<768;k++) s += lnbuf[i*768+k]*W[attn.w + k*2304+o]; qkv[i*2304+o]=s+W[attn.b+o];}
  // split q,k,v: q[i][o]=qkv[i*2304+o]; k=...+768; v=...+1536
  // reshape to heads: for each head h, qh[i][d]=q[i][h*64+d]
  // scores
  for(h=0; h<12; h++){
    for(i=0;i<N;i++){
      float maxv=-1e30;
      for(j=0;j<=i;j++){
        float s=0;
        for(d=0;d<64;d++) s += q[i*768+h*64+d] * k[j*768+h*64+d];
        s /= sqrt(64); // scale
        att[i*N+j]=s;
        if(s>maxv) maxv=s;
      }
      // softmax over j<=i
      float esum=0;
      for(j=0;j<=i;j++){ att[i*N+j]=expf(att[i*N+j]-maxv); esum+=att[i*N+j]; }
      for(j=0;j<=i;j++){ att[i*N+j]/=esum; }
      // attend v
      for(d=0;d<64;d++){ float a=0; for(j=0;j<=i;j++) a+=att[i*N+j]*v[j*768+h*64+d]; out[i*768+h*64+d]=a; }
    }
  }
  // residual + proj
  for(i=0;i<N;i++) for(o=0;o<768;o++){float s=out[i*768+o]; /* no weight? weighted sum: */
     float s2=0; for(k=0;k<768;k++) s2 += out[i*768+k]*W[proj.w + k*768+o];
     x[i*768+o] += s2 + W[proj.b+o];
  }
  // rest of layer...
}
```

This is a lot of loops. Need to keep code compact.

Also note we don't need to store all q/k/v separately with transpose; we can index directly with `q[idx*768 + h*64 + d]`.

Let's estimate code size. This will be verbose; under 5000 bytes, we need to golf heavily: use short names, combined loops, `#define` macros, etc.

Let's think about memory buffers:
- `float *ln = malloc(1024*768*4)` ~3MB
- `float *qkv = malloc(1024*2304*4)` ~9.4MB
- `float *att = malloc(1024*1024*4)` ~4MB
- `float *out = malloc(1024*768*4)` ~3MB
- `float *mlp = ...` can reuse.

We can reuse `ln` buffer for mlp outputs etc.

Actually layers:
1. ln1 (norm of x) into `a`
2. qkv
3. attention → out
4. residual x += proj(out)
5. ln2 → a (reuse)
6. fc → h (3072 wide)
7. gelu
8. proj2 → h2 (768 wide), residual x += h2.

We can reuse `a` (768 wide) for ln1, ln2.

For BPE and tokens, we need the embedding lookup:
```
x[i][k] = W[wte + tok[i]*768 + k] + W[wpe + i*768 + k];
```

After all layers, final LN on x, then logits = x[N-1] @ wte.T: `for(v=0;v<50257;v++){float s=0; for(k=0;k<768;k++) s+=lnf_out[k]*W[wte + v*768+k];}` choose argmax.

That's 50k*768 = 38.6M mults per token generation. For 20 tokens = 772M, okay.

Memory caution under 5000 bytes code, using malloc for big buffers is fine.

Now the output: We must print the continuation. The expected output "continue the output under whatever GPT-2 would print for the next 20 tokens" — likely we print the tokenized text of the generated token ids. We need to **decode** token ids back to readable text (reverse byte-level mapping + UTF-8?).

GPT-2's sample output prints the decoded text of generated token ids: for each token id, decode its byte sequence (token string), then map back via `unicode_to_bytes` and output raw bytes.

So after each generated token, we should output its raw bytes (text). For initial input, if the program is supposed to "continue the output", maybe it should reproduce the input and then the continuation, or just print continuation? The prompt: "you should continue the output under whatever GPT-2 would print for the next 20 tokens." I think program should print the input (echo) followed by generated continuation? Actually "continue the output under whatever GPT-2 would print" suggests it should print the full text: input + 20 generated tokens. We'll print the input text then the decoded generated tokens.

So we need `unicode_to_bytes` inverse mapping: token string bytes (UTF-8 of byte-mapped chars) → original bytes. Since the token strings are the UTF-8 encoding of the mapped chars, decoding is: for each Unicode code point (parse UTF-8), find original byte using inverse btu map, output that byte.

Simpler: We can precompute for each token id its decoded output bytes (the raw text it represents). For id 0..255, decoded bytes = single byte `b`. For merged ids, decoded = concatenation of decoded children. So we can store decoded byte strings too.

But actually the merged token's decoded bytes are just the concatenation of its component tokens' decoded bytes. Since token id 256+r = A[r]+B[r] (in byte-mapped space), its decoded raw bytes = decode(A[r]) + decode(B[r]). We can precompute `decoded[id]` for all 50k tokens, or we can decode on the fly from `tokstr[id]` (the UTF-8 bytestring) using the inverse mapping.

Implementing inverse mapping: `btu` maps byte→cp; we want cp→byte. Since cp ranges 0..511 and mapping is bijective on that range, we can have `byte_of_cp[512]` table. Decode: parse `tokstr[id]` as UTF-8 to code points (each 1 or 2 bytes), then for each cp output `byte_of_cp[cp]`.

We can precompute `char *dec[50257]` during vocab load: for each merge r, `dec[256+r] = concat(dec[A_id(r)], dec[B_id(r)])`? But A[r]/B[r] are strings of mapped chars; we know their decoded forms if we can decode A[r] and B[r] from bytes. We can compute `dec` for all tokens recursively using merge dependencies? Actually merges' A and B are sequences of token ids? Since A[r] string is a token string (could be multi-char from earlier merges). To decode A[r], we'd need to map it to a token id (since A[r] appears in vocabulary as an existing token). But building full mapping string→id for 50k tokens and decoding may be complex.

Simpler: decode directly from token string via inverse mapping. Since token strings are short (max maybe 20 bytes), decode cost is small. For generated output, we decode each token string on the fly.

We'll write function `out_token(int id)`: takes `tokstr[id]` (UTF-8 bytes), decodes to raw text and writes to stdout using `byte_of_cp`.

Parsing UTF-8: For a byte sequence, iterate: if byte < 0x80 → cp = byte, i+=1. Else if (b&0xE0)==0xC0 → cp = ((b&0x1F)<<6)|(next&0x3F), i+=2. Since all mapped chars are <512, this suffices. Then `putchar(byte_of_cp[cp])`.

But wait `tokstr` for byte tokens 0..255: these are the UTF-8 of `chr(btu[b])`. For b in ASCII 33..126 etc., it's 1 byte = b's ASCII char. For mapped high chars, 2 bytes. So decode works.

For input BPE: initial pieces must be created from `btu[byte]` code point; we set id=byte, and `tokstr[id]` = UTF-8 of `btu[byte]`. That matches.

Now the BPE merge matching compares `tokstr` byte strings against A/B (which are also UTF-8 byte strings). Good.

Wait: A[r] and B[r] are the byte strings as they appear in vocab.bpe (UTF-8). Their `id` is irrelevant; we just keep them as strings for merging. The merged token's string is `A[r]+B[r]` concatenated in byte space. This exactly equals `tokstr[256+r]`. So we don't even need to precompute `tokstr` for merged tokens separately—we can store `tokstr[256+r] = A[r]+B[r]`.

But careful: A[r] and B[r] strings themselves are in the merged/baseline vocabulary; e.g., A[r] could be a previously merged multi-char token. Its string appears in vocab.bpe as a line part. For example a later merge might use `Ġt` (merged from Ġ and t) as left part. The string `Ġt` appears as part of some line. So `tokstr` for that token (if it's an earlier merge result) equals that string. We can build `tokstr` for all ids 0..255 (byte strings) and 256+r = A[r]+B[r]. Good.

Now, the BPE loop needs to know which adjacent pair matches a merge. Since merge files are in rank order, the first matching r for pair (idX,idY) is the best (lowest rank). Good.

One more subtlety: The `#version: 0.2` first line should be skipped. In `encoder.py`, lines starting with '#' are skipped. We'll skip if first char '#'.

Hmm inverse mapping: we need cp→byte. For cp directly in 0..255 with inv[cp]=byte (for cp in 33..126 etc., inv[cp]=cp). For cp=256+n → byte = original unassigned byte. Let's build:

Approach: create arrays `byte_of_cp[512]` init 0.
For byte b in 33..126: byte_of_cp[b]=b.
For byte b in 161..172: byte_of_cp[b]=b.
For byte b in 174..255: byte_of_cp[b]=b.
For remaining bytes (not assigned), assign new cp starting at 256:
```
int next=256;
for(b=0;b<256;b++){
  if(!assigned(b)) { byte_of_cp[next]=b; next++; }
}
```
Then to convert byte→cp, we need reverse map cp_to_byte. We can compute `cp_byte[512]` (cp→byte) and use it both ways: for init, cp = cp_byte index? We need byte→cp table too. Let's define:
- `int c2b[512];` (cp→byte)
- For byte b: we need `cp = the codepoint mapped to b`. Let's instead compute `int b2c[256];` (byte→cp).
Build:
```
for(b=0;b<256;b++) b2c[b]= -1;
for b in r1: b2c[b]=b;
for b in r2: b2c[b]=b;
int next=256;
for b in 0..255: if(b2c[b]==-1) { b2c[b]=next; next++; }
```
Then c2b[b2c[b]]=b for all b.
Now token bytes for byte b: UTF-8 encode cp=b2c[b].

Let's write compactly:
```
unsigned char cp[256]; short b2[512];
// fill
for(i=0;i<256;i++)cp[i]=255;
for(i=33;i<=126;i++)cp[i]=i;

for(i=174;i<=255;i++)cp[i]=i;

for(i=0;i<256;i++)b2[cp[i]]=i;
```
`cp[i]` = byte→codepoint (0..511). `b2[cp]` = cp→byte.

Generate `bstr[b]` as UTF-8 string of cp[b]:
```
char *mk(int cp){ static char s[3]; if(cp<128){s[0]=cp;s[1]=0;} else {s[0]=0xC0|(cp>>6);s[1]=0x80|(cp&63);s[2]=0;} return s;}

Now load vocab:
```
FILE*f=fopen(av[2],"rb"); fseek(f,0,SEEK_END); long z=ftell(f); fseek(f,0,SEEK_SET); char*buf=malloc(z+1); fread(buf,1,z,f); fclose(f); buf[z]=0;

`strchr` on `ln` may find the first space; since tokens are byte-mapped strings without spaces (spaces encoded as 'Ġ'), the first space is the separator. Good.

Build `T` (token strings) for all ids:
```

The last token `<|endoftext|>` id 50256; `T[50256]` can be "..." or empty. We won't generate it with greedy unless logit picks it; but its `T` string should be `<|endoftext|>` for decode if it occurs. Let's set `T[50256]="<|endoftext|>"`.

Also decoded output: on decode, parse T[id] UTF-8 → c2b.

Tokenize function:
```
int tok(char*s,int*ids){
 int n=0;
 while(s[n]){ ids[n]= (unsigned char)s[n]; n++; }
 // convert each byte to byte-mapped init pieces. Actually initial piece id = byte value; its string is bstr[byte]. Good.
 while(1){
   int best=-1,bi=-1,r,i;
   for(i=0;i+1<n;i++){
     for(r=0;r<50000;r++){
       if(!strcmp(T[ids[i]],A[r]) && !strcmp(T[ids[i+1]],B[r])){ if(best<0||r<best){best=r;bi=i;} break; }
     }
   }
   if(best<0||n==1) break;
   ids[bi]=256+best;
   memmove(ids+bi+1, ids+bi+2, (n-bi-2)*4);
   n--;
 }
 return n;
}
```
Wait: for finding best, scanning r from 0 gives the lowest-rank match per pair; we take global min across pairs. Good.

But the `break` inside the r loop breaks on first matching r for that pair. Since r iterates ascending from 0, the first match is the best rank for that pair. Then we compare to global `best`. Good.

This is O(n * 50000) per merge. For n ~ 20, fine. For longer input maybe slow but okay.

Potential issue: `strcmp(T[ids[i]], A[r])` — `T[ids[i]]` for byte pieces is `bstr[byte]` which may contain NUL? Token strings are byte strings of UTF-8, no NUL inside. Fine.

Let's write forward:

```
void fw(int*ids,int n,int*out){
  float *x=..., *a=..., *q=..., *k=..., *v=..., *at=..., *o=...;
  // embedding
  for(i=0;i<n;i++){
    for(j=0;j<E;j++)
      x[i*E+j]=W[85842432 + ids[i]*E + j] + W[85056000 + i*E + j];
  }
  for(l=0;l<L;l++){
    int bb=l*7087872;
    // ln1
    for(i=0;i<n;i++){
      float mu=0,sd=0;
      for(j=0;j<E;j++) mu+=x[i*E+j];
      mu/=E;
      for(j=0;j<E;j++){ float d=x[i*E+j]-mu; sd+=d*d; }
      sd=sqrtf(sd/E+1e-5f);
      for(j=0;j<E;j++) a[i*E+j]=(x[i*E+j]-mu)/sd*W[bb+2363136+j]+W[bb+2362368+j];
    }
    // qkv
    for(i=0;i<n;i++)
      for(o=0;o<2304;o++){
        float s=W[bb+o]; // bias
        for(j=0;j<E;j++) s+=a[i*E+j]*W[bb+2304+j*2304+o];
        q[i*2304+o]=sprogress;
      }
    // q = ids? Use q pointer = qkv+o? Let's define q=qkv, k=qkv+768, v=qkv+1536.
    // attention heads
    for(h=0;h<H;h++){
      for(i=0;i<n;i++){
        // compute scores j<=i
        float mx=-1e30;
        for(j=0;j<=i;j++){
          float s=0;
          for(d=0;d<64;d++) s+=q[i*2304+h*64+d] * q? no, k[i?]...
```
Careful: qkv layout: q at offset 0..767, k 768..1535, v 1536..2303. For position i, head h, dim d:
`q[i*2304 + h*64 + d]`
`k[j*2304 + 768 + h*64 + d]`
`v[j*2304 + 1536 + h*64 + d]`

Score = dot(q_i, k_j) / sqrt(64).

Softmax over j=0..i (causal mask, no masking needed because we only iterate j<=i). Then output o[i*E+h*64+d] = sum_j softmax * v[j][h,d].

Then proj:
```
for(i=0;i<n;i++) for(o=0;o<E;o++){
  float s=0;
  for(j=0;j<E;j++) s+=o[i*E+j]*W[bb+1772544+j*E+o];
  x[i*E+o]=x[i*E+o]+s+W[bb+1771776+o];
}
```
Then ln2 (similar to ln1 but g/b at 2364672/2363904).

MLP:
```
for(i=0;i<n;i++) for(o=0;o<F;o++){
  float s=W[bb+2365440+o];
  for(j=0;j<E;j++) s+=a[i*E+j]*W[bb+2368512+j*F+o];
  a? store in mlp buffer h[i*F+o] = gelu(s)
}
for(i=0;i<n;i++) for(o=0;o<E;o++){
  float s=W[bb+4727808+o];
  for(j=0;j<F;j++) s+=h[i*F+j]*W[bb+4728576+j*E+o];
  x[i*E+o]=x[i*E+o]+s;
}
```
GELU exact: `0.5*x*(1+tanh(sqrt(2/PI)*(x+0.044715*x^3)))`. GPT-2 uses exact GELU. For arg-max sampling, approximate GELU is fine as long as ordering roughly preserved, but to match exactly, we should use exact. Under <5000 bytes, we could use `tanh` approximation. Let's include `#include <math.h>` and use exact GELU:
`0.5f*x*(1.0f+tanhf(0.7978845608f*(x+0.044715f*x*x*x)))`.

After L layers:
```
// ln_f
for(i=0;i<n;i++){ // only need last position for logits but norm uses x rows independently
  mu, sd over E;
  for(j) a[i]=(x[i*E+j]-mu)/sd*W[85055232+j]+W[85054464+j];
}
// logits from last position a[n-1]
int best=0; float bv=-1e30;
for(v=0;v<VO;v++){
  float s=0;
  for(j=0;j<E;j++) s+=a[(n-1)*E+j]*W[85842432+v*E+j];
  if(s>bv){bv=s;best=v;}
}
*out=best;
```

That's the model.

Decoding function:

void putt(int id){
  unsigned char*p=(unsigned char*)T[id];
  while(*p){
    int cp;
    if(*p<128){cp=*p++;}else{cp=((p[0]&31)<<6)|(p[1]&63);p+=2;}
    putchar(inv[cp]);
  }
}
```
where `inv[512]` = cp→byte.
